> **Course 26, Engineering Design and Professional Practice, is under way** (Term 7; seven chapters, 26-01 to 26-07, about 40 hours). 26-01 (Requirements, Specifications and the Design Process) is verified: MRD → PRD → architecture and block specifications; verifiable requirements (criterion, conditions, method), shall/will/should, TBD/TBR and the four NASA verification methods; verification against validation; flow-down by duty-cycle averages, worst-case and RSS stacking with correlated terms and the Gaussian out-of-spec fraction; traceability matrices and the expected cost of gaps; waterfall (with what Royce actually wrote), V-model, spiral, phase-gate and agile, and why chips gate at specification freeze and tape-out; the escalation model $C = N[p_1c_1 + (1 - p_1)\bar c_{down}]$ with the NASA ranges; weighted decision matrices and the tie weight. 6 examples, 15 problems with solutions, verify (215 checks: an explicit timeline integral for battery life, Monte Carlo and exact convolution for budgets, an absorbing Markov chain and a backward recursion for escalation, root-finding for tie weights), lab (`reqs.py`, notebook), 2 figures, 13 glossary terms, R-625 to R-629 (NASA NTRS, NASA SE Handbook Rev 2, Royce 1970, Boehm 1988 via Crossref, the Agile Manifesto, the 2024 Wilson report, the 29148 preview, all read from primary documents through the session proxy with curl; the WebFetch tool could not resolve these hosts).
>
> 26-02 (Design Reviews, Documentation and Standards Bodies) is verified: peer and milestone reviews (NASA definitions) mapped to chip gates with entry and exit criteria; detection by $k$ reviewers, the cost-optimal team size and the ceiling set by shared blind spots; capture–recapture (Lincoln–Petersen, Chapman) and its low bias under heterogeneous difficulty; baselines, change control, datasheets and errata; how standards are made (IEEE SA ballot thresholds, 3GPP release freezes, PCIe and HBM releases, UCIe promoters); FRAND (ETSI 6.1), IEEE's reasonable-rate and prohibitive-order language, royalty stacking and the royalty base; designing to a draft standard. 6 examples, 12 problems, verify (125 checks: simulated reviewers, hypergeometric MLE, exhaustive ballot searches), lab (`reviews.py`, notebook), 1 figure, 13 glossary terms, R-630 to R-635 (all read from primary documents; JEDEC and PCI-SIG pages return bot challenges and were not read, so their facts come from earlier logged secondary sources R-273 and R-474).
>
> 26-03 (Engineering Economics I: NRE, Die Cost, ASP and Gross Margin) is verified: the cascaded cost stack (yields divide accumulated cost), gross margin and markup; NRE amortization, fully loaded cost and break-even volume; the FE interest factors derived from the geometric series, capital recovery with salvage, effective and continuous rates, NPV, IRR, payback, EUAC and straight-line depreciation; a chip program's NPV with a slip and a closing market window; tornado sensitivity. 6 examples, 15 problems, verify (138 checks: factors by explicit summation, lot accounting for the cost stack, Brent against bisection for IRR, directly built slipped cash flows), lab (`econ.py`, notebook), 1 figure, 16 glossary terms, R-636 (NCEES econ topics) and R-637 (NVIDIA FY2026 margins), with R-575, R-579 and R-623 reused.
>
> 26-04 (Engineering Economics II: Build, Buy or License; Node; Chiplets; ASIC vs Merchant) is verified: incremental analysis and the discounted unit stream; licensing against building with expected slip cost, the discounted break-even and who carries the risk (operating leverage); per-unit, percentage, tiered and device-base royalties, price erosion and stacking; node choice by the mix-weighted (harmonic) density gain, "Amdahl's law for area", cost per good function and the NRE break-even with a yield ramp; monolithic against chiplets as a family decision (mixed nodes and reuse); custom accelerators against merchant parts by TCO per unit of throughput, the break-even fleet and the merchant price ceiling; decision trees and the value of flexibility. 6 examples, 16 problems, verify (263 checks: backward-stepped present values, root-finding for every break-even and threshold, lot accounting for die and package costs, joule-integrated energy, enumeration of every decision-tree policy), lab (`sourcing.py`, which imports 26-03's `econ.py`; notebook), 1 figure, 22 glossary terms, R-638 to R-644 (Arm, SoftBank, Synopsys, Cadence via republication, Broadcom, Marvell, hyperscaler accelerator announcements), with R-321, R-360, R-464, R-575 to R-577 and R-637 reused.
>
> 26-05 (Schedule and Risk: Why Respins Happen) is verified: the shape of a chip schedule (verification share, fab cycle time per mask layer); PERT three-point estimates, path statistics, the normal approximation and merge bias, with a beta-PERT Monte Carlo showing the approximation is optimistic; expected tape-outs from per-spin success probabilities and the survey data behind them; the ladder of fixes (workaround, trim, metal-only ECO with spare cells and held wafers, full respin, recall); risk registers, EMV and mitigation under a budget; when to tape out from an exponential bug-discovery model with Poisson escapes (the cost ridge and the minimum where $\lambda e^{-\lambda} = c_w\tau/C_r$), and the value of emulation and formal verification; margins as mitigation. 6 examples, 13 problems, verify (181 checks: beta-PERT means from scipy, erf-based normal probabilities, exact numerical convolution against the Monte Carlo, full-distribution expectations, on/off enumeration of mitigation sets, direct minimization and maximization of the expected cost), lab (`risk.py`, notebook), 1 figure, 9 glossary terms, R-645 to R-648 (the 2024 Wilson/Siemens report with chart readings, NVIDIA's Blackwell mask change, Intel's Sapphire Rapids validation delays and production stepping, the ISPD 2008 spare-cell paper and vendor ECO pages), with R-018, R-020 and R-321 reused.
>
> 26-06 (Engineering Team Structures and Sizing) is verified: the disciplines that build a chip and their staffing profiles (verification-to-design ratios from the 2022 Wilson study); communication channels and a coordination-cost model with the best flat team size $n^* = (1 + 1/c)/2$ and the hierarchy that escapes it; Brooks's law with ramp-up and mentoring, and when hiring helps; functional, program, matrix and platform organizations; span of control and layers; employees, contractors and design services (the newsvendor rule, break-even utilization); converting design-cost estimates to engineer-years. 6 examples, 12 problems, verify (137 checks: math.comb and pair enumeration, brute-force and bounded optimization, a time-stepped work burn-down, integer-ceiling layer counts, brute-force staffing bases), lab (`team.py`, notebook), 1 figure, 11 glossary terms, R-649 to R-653 (Conway 1968, Brooks via a course-hosted scan, the 2022 Wilson Part 8 blog, GUC, Socionext and Alchip results, NVIDIA's Volta statement), with R-017, R-018, R-019 and R-642/643 reused. >
> Course 25 audit lessons to carry forward:
>
> - **(i) Print the constants you compute with.** Results printed to six digits (kT = 0.109712 eV) need $k$ = 8.61733 × 10⁻⁵ eV/K, not 8.6173 or 8.617; check that a reader using the printed constant reproduces every printed digit.
> - **(ii) A formula on the sheet must match the chapter's definition.** The exam and sheet called $n\lambda/(2\text{NA}^2)$ the depth of focus while 25-02 defines DOF as twice that.
> - **(iii) Every figure must be in the log entry it cites.** ASML's €32.7 B cited R-570 and R-563, neither of which held it; it was removed.
> - **(iv) Exams need inverse parts and must not echo flashcards.** Nine exam parts repeated chapter problems or examples, and five flashcards printed exam answers; the exam now has inverse parts (oxide for a silicon depth, projected range for a junction, BOX for a coupling, burn-in time, defect density for a claimed saving).
> - **(v) State every model parameter an exam question needs.** Question 12(a) said "Example 25-07.9's costs" in a closed-book exam; it now lists them.
>
> Tips carried from Course 22: switched-converter ngspice runs show sub-millivolt steps when the time step changes, so measure averages over a late window and peak-to-peak values over the final period only; new notebooks that call ngspice must use the guarded helper (copied in 21-01 to 21-10), because CI's notebook job has no ngspice; `.meas integ` on non-uniform transient points is inaccurate for small harmonics, so run, `linearize`, then `meas` in a `.control` block.
>
> Course 22 audit lessons to carry forward:
>
> - **(i) State the boundary of every efficiency and saving.** PUE is facility power over IT power metered after the UPS, so multiplying the UPS input by the PUE counts the UPS loss twice. The exam's two "150 W" savings add on 22-10's regulator-only boundary (about 349 W) but overlap if the baseline was measured at the die.
> - **(ii) Label a rule of thumb as a rule, and check it against the exact optimum.** The droop-minimizing ESR is 0.53 $Z_0$ by simulation, against the $Z_0/2$ rule; the optimum is flat, so the rule stands, but the question must say which it asks for.
> - **(iii) Nonlinear capacitance needs charge, not energy.** Both the hard-turn-on loss and the ZVS energy condition are $Q_{oss}V$ ($C_{o(tr)}V^2$), which equals $2E_{oss}$ only for a linear capacitor.
> - **(iv) Exams must not reuse problem-set setups either.** Q11(b) was Problem 22-11.3 exactly, and Q3(c) shared Problem 22-03.2's duty cycle; check originality against the problem sets as well as the worked examples.
> - **(v) Size the exam from its keyed answers.** 270 answers with four derivations did not fit 240 minutes; the paper now runs 330 minutes in two sittings.
> - **(vi) Specify the loss model in the question.** "The inductor and switch together have 50 mΩ" admits two models; state the winding resistance and which devices are ideal.
> - **(vii) Every intermediate a solution prints is a published number.** Key it with its part label, or drop it (Ex 22-06.4's 0.32 and 3.6, Solution 22-06.9's Baliga products, the figure's 208 and 544 kHz crossings).
>
> Course 21 audit lessons to carry forward:
>
> - **(i) Define every figure of merit once, then use it consistently.** Examples: terminated-input noise figure $F = 2F_A$; single-balanced mixer gain $2/\pi$; the two-tone compression offset $10\log_{10}3$; PA output power, which is proportional to $I_1$ at full swing, so it is compared in 10 log, not 20 log.
> - **(ii) Derive closed forms by a second route.**
>   - The $G_A$ maximization is a quadratic; $G_{max} = \lvert S_{21}/S_{12}\rvert/(K + \sqrt{K^2 - 1})$ needs the stable form to avoid cancellation.
>   - The DS-TWR estimate is $2ab\tau/(a + b)$; verify it with sympy.
>   - The class-F3 maximum is 90.69 % at $V_3 = V_1/6$.
> - **(iii) Read the governing table, not a summary of a later draft.** The 802.11ax sensitivities were cited to an 802.11be white paper. The two TR 38.821 inputs were missing from the research log until the 3GPP archive copy was read.
> - **(iv) Dimensioned words need numbers that agree everywhere.** For example, the package-routing loss read "fraction of a dB" in one place and "decibels" in another, and the real-user share of peak rate was given three ways.
> - **(v) A sanity check must hold at every point it covers.** The squint "more than half the beamwidth" rule failed at 30 GHz.
> - **(vi) Rescale small physical variables before brentq.** Its default absolute xtol of 2e-12 is too coarse at fs and fF scales.
> - **(vii) State model limits where an answer lands near them.** One answer of 3.9 m sits below a model fitted for 4–30 m.
> - **(viii) Figures must disclose their processing.** One figure had an undisclosed Hann window and a round-trip sweep model.
>
> Course 20 audit lessons to carry forward: (i) energy and noise limits must count each charge once (the class-B sampler's thermal line was double-counted: 4kT·SNR at Nyquist, not 8kT); (ii) check every schematic's loop polarity against the stated feedback sign (20-04 Figure 1 had the error amplifier's inputs reversed); (iii) sign-check second-order corrections against a simulation (R-2R switch resistance makes the MSB DNL negative); (iv) a claim of exactness needs the model to carry the physics (output-impedance harmonics treated a capacitive impedance as resistive); (v) derivations whose result depends on which parasitic loads which node must say so (MDAC $C_{L,eff}$); (vi) diligence verdicts must compare like with like (the exam's ADC claim against the right front end); (vii) evidence tags only from [Established], [Industry estimate], [Contested]; (viii) number figures in reading order; (ix) a lab handout must quote what its notebook prints, and explain any gap between a grid search and a continuous optimum.
>
> Course 19 audit lessons to carry forward: (i) attribute every number to the right part of its source: SkyWater's page gives `hd` "160 kGates/mm^2 or better", and the 120 kGates/mm² that R-317 had logged for `hd` belongs to `hdll`; read a multi-section page section by section; (ii) re-read the primary table rather than a rounded summary (IRDS CV/I 1.061 → 0.624 ps is a factor of 1.70, not the 1.71 of rounded inputs); (iii) give scaled and unscaled quantities different names and say which is meant (Elmore constant τ_E vs Elmore estimate 0.69τ_E; Liberty slope R_eff = ln 2 · R_eq vs the resistor R_eq; utilization u vs clock uncertainty U); (iv) one symbol per quantity across a course (V_TH threshold, V_T = kT/q; reference/notation.md now lists the Course 19 timing and layout symbols); (v) print an equation's validity range where it is used, and solve examples region by region when they leave it (19-08's write level); (vi) problem sets must exercise every stated objective, including writing constraints (SDC), false paths, half-cycle paths, partitioning, complex and skewed gates, device-level capacitances and fall times; (vii) when the text prints a rounded coefficient (0.69, 0.38) but computes with ln 2 and 0.379, say so once and show ln 2 in displayed arithmetic.
>
> Course 18 audit lessons to carry forward: (i) a bound that is correct for the common case can be wrong in the general one (the k-buffer block size needed a throughput floor that only binds for k ≥ 3); test models at the edges, not only at the worked example; (ii) a lab can confirm a wrong explanation (the ring buffer's `dmb` on one core; a verify simulation that built in the √2 it was meant to test): ask what the check could *not* detect; (iii) one-directional analyses hide failures (a UART link checked only with the MCU transmitting); (iv) business cases need the realistic alternative, not a strawman (single slot with a DFU bootloader); (v) code-writing problems need a mechanical checker: host tests with injected interrupts and mutation checks now back two of them; (vi) answer-key IDs collide when appending (three collisions caught this session): grep before adding.
>
> Course 16 and 17 audit lessons to carry forward:
> (a) verify checks that restate a formula's own output catch nothing; derive the value another way (17-03 now finds spectral lines by FFT instead of hard-coding them);
> (b) Monte Carlo checks need their own random generator, or unrelated edits shift them (17-08's Viterbi check moved from 0.5 to 0.9 of the bound when earlier code changed), and their tolerance must match the real scatter (independent million-bit runs gave 0.35–1.0 of the bound);
> (c) hand arithmetic in solutions slips even when the keyed result is right (a long-division trace and a bisection bracket were wrong); generate traces by code;
> (d) a lab handout's expected results must match what its notebook prints, and be checked;
> (e) prose errors dominate: array vs diversity gain, BP described as ML, a Viterbi correction claim, an "entropy-limited" quantizer; reviewers also flagged missing derivations (water-filling, maximum entropy, Rayleigh), missing standard topics (BCH, BICM, interleaving, carrier recovery) and problem sets that did not exercise every objective;
> (f) an exam must not reuse worked examples; check its originality against the chapters;
> (g) fetch the primary text for every perishable number (polar mother-code lengths differ by channel in TS 38.212; UE REFSENS's reference channel is in TS 38.101-1 Annex A.3.2).
> (1) Prose claims need the same rigour as keyed numbers. The four Course 14 audits (A 21, B 26, C 21, D 25 findings) found almost nothing wrong in keyed values; the errors were in explanations (forwarding paths, small-tile cache mechanism, gshare history, warm-up causes), in unkeyed intermediate numbers, and in claims a log entry did not support.
> (2) When an explanation can be checked mechanically, check it: verify now asserts the forwarding source of each operand (the 14-06 testbench logs them), Solution 14-04.15's division trace, Solution 14-07.13's predictor trace, the Tomasulo and directory tables, the VLIW schedules (legal and proven optimal by exhaustive search) and the 14-14 skew diagram, all parsed from the published Markdown.
> (3) Models must state their assumptions where results depend on them: instant prefetches, constant-stride lists, Svadu A/D updates, misprediction recovery at resolution, write-allocate in roofline byte counts, the CDB arbitration rule.
> (4) Lab models must enforce what the chapter claims: the TLB now caches PTE flags and checks them on hits, and a lost-interrupt race in axil_regs.v was fixed with a collision test that the old RTL fails.
> (5) Version-dependent tool outputs (clang 18 instruction counts) are keyed and fail loudly if the tool version differs.
> (6) Primary sources fetched during the audit: OCP MX v1.1 (R-143), RISC-V CFI, Zkt, Sv57 petapages and LR/SC livelock freedom (R-201, R-213, R-215, R-218), Retbleed CVEs (R-213), Hopper clusters (R-220), TPU v4 SparseCores (R-222) and the first TPU's weight-stationary matrix unit (R-226).

If the scratchpad is gone, re-gather the facts for the chapter being written. New research-log entries start at R-088. Programming chapters put example code in fenced `python` blocks, each followed by a fenced `text` block with the exact output; the verify script calls `run_python_blocks` on the chapter and the solutions, so every printed output is machine-checked.
> - C labs (05-05 onward) use `tools/run_c.py`: every `*_test.c` is compiled with `-Werror` and AddressSanitizer/UndefinedBehaviorSanitizer and must print `TEST PASSED`; `*_demo.c` files (deliberate-bug demonstrations) are compiled only. CI runs it as the `c` job.
> - Lab discussion questions now carry worked answers (from 03-01 on). Keep doing this for every new lab, and backfill older labs (open issue 16).

Citations logged as R-256; R-237 and R-241 extended. Lessons: (1) verify scripts that restate a formula's own output cannot catch an interpretive error (the wrap count, the settling comparison); (2) count events in the simulated hardware, not in an idealized variable; (3) a 'minimum length' from an optimizer can depend on the solver's grid; (4) test vectors must cover both signs of every clamp; (5) a sum formula (Poisson) can hide a boundary term. |
